  table, so I'll treat any line with cell separators as a table row, not a
  heading. I'm turning loaders.py into a package with one module per format and
  a suffix-to-loader registry, so PDF support later only needs a module and one
  entry.

⏺ The HTML loader and the loader abstraction are done. All six filings are
  indexed, and the retrieval eval runs on the new questions. One "done when"
  item is still open: the Chrome extension wasn't connected, so I couldn't check
  a cited answer in the browser. I sent real questions through the API and the
  Vite /api proxy instead, which is the path the browser uses. The work is on
  branch feat/html-loader in 6 commits; nothing is pushed.

  Loader abstraction
  - ingestion/loaders/ is now a package with one function per format that takes
    the file's bytes and returns the text (text.py, html.py).
  - A suffix → loader table (LOADERS) is the single source of accepted types:
    uploads, the corpus filter and the 415 message all read from it.
  - PDF (task 8) only needs a pdf.py and one entry in that table. PageSpan is
    already in place for page numbers. I didn't write the PDF loader, since it's
    task 8.

  HTML loader, checked against the real filings
  - Hidden content: the hidden XBRL block (ix:header), anything with
    display:none, scripts, styles and comments are removed. No XBRL text is left
    in the output.
  - Words split across tags: inline elements are joined without adding spaces,
    because the filings split words across tags (MSFT's ITEM 1. B + USINESS).
    &nbsp; becomes a normal space.
  - Tables: each row becomes one line, with cells separated by |. A $, ) or % in
    its own cell is joined back to its number, so rows read like $(5,678).
  - Section detection: all three tables of contents are HTML tables with no dot
    leaders, so the TOC rows were being taken as headings. Detection now skips
    table rows, and finds all 23 Items in each filing.
  - Tables and chunks: 88% of HTML tables fit whole in one chunk, against 35%
    for TXT; 18% of HTML table chunks lack the header row, against 41%. These
    are different documents, so the comparison is rough, but HTML doesn't make
    the header problem worse.

  Other changes
  - Company names are now "Microsoft Corporation" and "NVIDIA Corporation" in
    the manifest, and in fetch_filings.py so a re-fetch keeps them.
  - The upload dialog accepts .htm and .html, and the example questions now
    cover the new corpus.
  - I rebuilt the local index; the old one is set aside in the scratchpad.
  - Existing Docker installs need docker compose down -v once, because an
    existing volume keeps its old manifest. This is in the README.

  Task 2: proposed approach

  I read QAService and the LLM interface. Three things in the current code shape
  the design:

  - LLMClient.complete() returns only a string, so the provider's token counts
    are thrown away.
  - Answer doesn't say why there is no answer. A relevance-gate block, an
    INSUFFICIENT_EVIDENCE reply and a reply discarded for having no valid
    citations all look the same.
  - The budget is tight. About 50 questions need the LLM (the 3 off-topic ones
    stop at the gate), at roughly 3,000 tokens each, so about 150k tokens per
    run. At 8k tokens a minute that's 20+ minutes, and it's close to Groq's
    daily cap. A run may not finish in one go.

  Design

  1. Two steps: ask, then score.
  - run_answer_eval.py ask calls the real model and appends one JSON line per
    question to a raw file, e.g. eval/answer_runs/2026-11-08-gpt-oss-120b.jsonl.
  - run_answer_eval.py score reads that file and writes eval/answer_results.md,
    without asking again.
  - Scoring rules, expected answers and later the figure check (task 3) can then
    change and be re-scored at no token cost.

  2. Resumable and paced.
  - Each raw line holds the question id, answer, cited and retrieved chunks
    (filing, section, chunk id, ranks), timings, token usage, refusal reason,
    plus a header with the model, settings and git commit.
  - ask skips questions already in the file.
  - It paces by the tokens actually used in the last 60 seconds.
  - On a 429 or daily-cap error it stops cleanly instead of retrying, and you
    resume later. --only a21,m22 lets you re-ask single questions.

  3. Two small changes to the app code (both useful outside the eval):
  - complete() returns a Completion(text, input_tokens, output_tokens) instead
    of a string. Both SDKs report usage, and the change touches the two
    adapters, FakeLLM and the service.
  - Answer gets a refusal field: gate, model or no_citations. The API can expose
    it later.

  4. An expected-answer field in questions.jsonl. It is a list of regex
  patterns, for example ["\\$?254,098 million", "\\$?254\\.1 billion"]. The
  first pattern must occur in the filing, and validate() enforces that the same
  way it checks evidence. The others are accepted ways of writing the same fact,
  which you approve. This keeps billions-versus-millions matching in the open,
  not hidden in code.

  5. What gets reported (per question set: TXT filings, HTML filings,
  unanswerable):
  - Answered: answerable questions that got an answer.
  - Correct: the answer matches one of the expected patterns.
  - Evidence cited: a cited source from the filing the question names contains
    the evidence pattern.
  - Answered from the other filing: an old question answered with the 2026
    figure. Task 1 showed this happens, and it isn't simply wrong.
  - Correct refusals, split into gate, model and no citations.
  - Latency (median and 95th percentile) and tokens.
  - A figure-check column, empty until task 3.

  Plus a per-question table, so every result can be read and checked by hand.

  6. Tests. Scoring is pure functions over the saved records, unit-tested with
  synthetic records. Pacing and resuming are tested with FakeLLM and a fake
  clock. No network in CI.

  Decisions I need from you

  1. Change complete() to return a Completion, or count tokens only in the eval?
     The eval-only option would be a wrapper that estimates tokens with a
     tokenizer, which is less accurate. I recommend the Completion.
  2. Who writes the expected answers? I'd write them for all 45 answerable
     questions and check each against the filing text, then you approve them
     together with the 2026 questions still pending.
  3. One run, or repeated runs? The temperature is 0.1, so answers vary between
     runs. I'd do one full run now and keep a --repeat option for later, budget
     permitting.

  If you agree, I'll start a new branch for task 2. Should it branch from
  feat/html-loader, since it needs the new questions, or from main once you've
  merged that?

❯ For the evaluation what metrics do we use? I didn't see Recall@20 for each    
  retriever before fusion, I think that might be a good metric                  

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ We already measure each retriever on its own, but only down to rank 10.
  Recall@20 is a good addition because it matches the system: each retriever
  passes its top 20 candidates to fusion (retrieval_candidates = 20). If the
  evidence isn't in either top 20, fusion can't recover it.

  What we measure today

  Retrieval (eval/run_eval.py → eval/results.md, no LLM calls):
  - R@1, 3, 6, 8, 10 and MRR, for hybrid, dense alone and BM25 alone. This is
    broken down by chunk size (200, 300, 400 tokens) and by filing format (HTM
    or TXT). A passage only counts if it comes from the filing the question
    names.
  - Relevance gate: the best dense similarity for answerable, off-topic and
    in-domain unanswerable questions, compared with MIN_RELEVANCE.
  - Questions missed: each question not found in the top 6, with its rank.

  Answers (eval/run_answer_eval.py, uses the LLM):
  - answered or refused, and why it refused (gate, model or no citations)
  - correct, meaning every expected fact appears in the answer
  - the evidence passage was cited
  - the answer cited only the other year's filing
  - latency and token counts

  What Recall@20 per retriever would show

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Metric         │                What it tells us                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Dense R@20, BM25 R@20 │ How often each retriever finds the evidence at  │
  │                       │ all                                             │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Union R@20 (in either │ The best hybrid can possibly do                 │
  │  list)                │                                                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Union R@20 − hybrid   │ Evidence that was retrieved but lost in fusion  │
  │ R@6                   │ or the cut to 6                                 │
  └───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The current table already hints at a problem this would explain. At 200
  tokens, dense alone beats hybrid at R@6: 0.88 against 0.83 for HTM, and 0.90
  against 0.81 for TXT. BM25 is much weaker (0.67 and 0.57). It looks like RRF
  lets weak BM25 hits push good dense hits out of the top 6. Recall@20 would
  tell us which of two problems we have:
  - Fusion is losing evidence: the union finds it but the fused top 6 drops it.
    The fix would be RRF weights or fewer BM25 candidates.
  - The candidates miss it: neither top 20 contains it. The fix would be in
    chunking or the embedder.

  How I'd build it
  - Add 20 to the depths reported in the per-retriever rows.
  - Add a union row computed from the two candidate lists.
  - Write a short note in decisions.md on what the numbers show.

  It needs no LLM calls, about 15 minutes of compute across the three chunk
  sizes, and a couple of unit tests.

  Since PR #2 is still open, I'd do this on its own branch from main once PR #2
  is merged, and before the table-column fix. That way the fix gets measured
  with the new metric too. Should I go ahead?

      104 +@dataclass(frozen=True)                                       
      105 +class CandidateRecall:                                        
      106 +    """Which questions had their evidence found, by question i
          +d."""                                                         
      107 +                                                              
      108 +    total: int                                                
      109 +    dense: frozenset[str]                                     
      110 +    sparse: frozenset[str]                                    
      111 +    hybrid: frozenset[str]                                    
      112 +                                                              
      113 +    @property                                                 
      114 +    def either(self) -> frozenset[str]:                       
      115 +        return self.dense | self.sparse                       
      116 +                                                              
      117 +    @property                                                 
      118 +    def dense_only(self) -> frozenset[str]:                   
      119 +        return self.dense - self.sparse                       
      120 +                                                              
      121 +    @property                                                 
      122 +    def sparse_only(self) -> frozenset[str]:                  
      123 +        return self.sparse - self.dense                       
      124 +                                                              
      125 +    @property                                                 
      126 +    def lost_by_fusion(self) -> frozenset[str]:               
      127 +        """Found by a retriever, but not in the fused top k.""
          +"                                                             
      128 +        return self.either - self.hybrid                      
      129 +                                                              
      130 +                                                              
      131 +def candidate_recall(                                         
      132 +    ids: Sequence[str], ranks: dict[str, Sequence[int | None]]
          +, candidates: int, top_k: int                                 
      133 +) -> CandidateRecall:                                         
      134 +    """`ranks` holds each mode's first-hit rank per question, 
          +in the order of `ids`."""                                     
      135 +                                                              
      136 +    def found(mode: str, depth: int) -> frozenset[str]:       
      137 +        hits = zip(ids, ranks[mode], strict=True)             
      138 +        return frozenset(i for i, r in hits if r is not None a
          +nd r <= depth)                                                
      139 +                                                              
      140 +    return CandidateRecall(                                   
      141 +        total=len(ids),                                       
      142 +        dense=found("dense", candidates),                     
      143 +        sparse=found("sparse", candidates),                   
      144 +        hybrid=found("hybrid", top_k),                        
      145 +    )

           : float) -> int:

           arities)

  Created PR #3, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I added Recall@20 for each retriever, plus a "Candidates before fusion" table,
  to the retrieval eval. Most of what hybrid misses is found by one of the
  retrievers and then dropped by the fusion step. It's up as PR #3:
  https://github.com/18ivan18/novabridge-qa/pull/3

  The checks pass and I re-ran the retrieval eval. MRR still stops at rank 10,
  so it stays comparable with earlier runs.

  At 400 tokens (our setting)

  ┌───────────┬────────────┬───────────┬─────────────────┬────────────┐
  │ Questions │ Dense R@20 │ BM25 R@20 │ Found by either │ Hybrid R@6 │
  ├───────────┼────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ HTML (24) │ 0.92       │ 0.83      │ 0.96            │ 0.83       │
  ├───────────┼────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ TXT (21)  │ 0.81       │ 0.90      │ 0.90            │ 0.76       │
  └───────────┴────────────┴───────────┴─────────────────┴────────────┘

  - Hybrid misses 9 of the 45 questions in the top 6, and 6 of them were found 
    by a retriever. Only a01, n04 and m25 were missed by both, which is a
    candidate problem.
  - The cause is how fusion (RRF) adds up scores. A passage found by both
    retrievers always outranks one found by only one:
    - Found by both, even at rank 20 in each list, it scores at least 2/80 =
      0.025.
    - Found by one, even at rank 1, it scores at most 1/61 ≈ 0.016.

    So when the two lists share six or more passages, nothing found by only one
    retriever reaches the top 6. Every passage that only BM25 found, at every
    chunk size, was dropped this way.
  - What it means for task 4 (re-ranker): re-ranking all the candidates could
    raise R@6 to 0.96 and 0.90. Weighting the two retrievers wouldn't fix it,
    because the problem is the adding, not the weights. I've written these
    numbers into task 4's "Why" in IMPROVEMENTS.md, and into decisions.md and
    the README.

  Run-to-run variance. At 200 tokens, one question (m06) fell from dense rank 1
  to 18 in this run, so dense R@6 on the TXT questions went from 0.90 to 0.86.
  When I rebuilt the index six more times, it came back at rank 1 every time,
  and I didn't find the cause. Chroma's index search is approximate, and its top
  20 differs from an exact search for 3–4 of the 53 questions in each build. I
  kept the run as measured rather than re-running for a better number, and noted
  in decisions.md that a one-question change at 200 tokens is noise. The 300
  and 400 token rows match the previous run exactly.

  Decision for you: after you merge this, I'd still fix the HTML table-column
  bug next, because it gives wrong numbers with citations. The re-ranker (task
  4) would come right after. Or I can do the re-ranker first if you'd rather.

⏺ One catch: the relevance gate takes the best dense similarity among the final
  top 6. Re-ranking changes which passages are in that top 6, and passages only
  BM25 found have no dense similarity. So the gate's behaviour would shift even
  if I left it alone. I'll decide how to handle that once the benchmark shows
  how re-ranker scores separate off-topic questions from answerable ones.

✻ Worked for 2m 25s · done Sunday 11:14 PM

❯ What do you suggest, what are our options?                                    

⏺ Benchmark results aren't in yet. Here are the options for the relevance gate.

  A. Keep the dense gate, but compute it before re-ranking (my default)
  - The gate would use the best dense similarity among all the candidates, not
    just the final top 6. That's almost always the passage dense search ranks
    first, which today's top 6 nearly always includes.
  - The gate would then behave as it does today, with the same MIN_RELEVANCE
    0.58 and the same off-topic margin (0.528 against 0.58).
  - It doesn't depend on which re-ranker we pick, so swapping models never means
    retuning.
  - Downside: it doesn't use the stronger relevance signal.

  B. Replace it with a re-ranker score threshold
  - A cross-encoder reads the question and the passage together. It could also
    block the 5 in-domain unanswerable questions, which the dense gate can't;
    today the model refuses those itself.
  - But fastembed returns raw scores that aren't calibrated across models. The
    threshold would need retuning for each model.
  - We'd be tuning it on only 3 off-topic and 5 in-domain unanswerable
    questions, which is easy to overfit.
  - If the threshold is too high, it refuses answerable questions. That's the
    costlier mistake, since a needless refusal is a lost answer, while the model
    already refuses unanswerable questions correctly.

  C. Require both (dense ≥ 0.58 and re-ranker ≥ t)
  - This is the most conservative option. It adds a second threshold, and its
    refusals are the union of A's and B's.

  My suggestion: build A as part of this task, since it keeps the current
  behaviour exactly. The benchmark already records each model's best re-ranker
  score for every answerable and unanswerable question. Then:
  - Clear margin: if the chosen model separates them clearly, including the
    in-domain unanswerable questions, I'd propose B with the numbers, as a
    separate decision.
  - No clear margin: we stay on A and record "not worth it" in decisions.md, as
    the task asks.

  Two other choices the benchmark will settle:
  - Re-rank 20 or 40 candidates: re-ranking the top 20 fused passages caps R@6
    at 0.92 / 0.81, while all 40 caps it at 0.96 / 0.90. 40 costs about twice
    the CPU time.
  - Text given to the model: with the title and section header we index with, or
    the plain chunk. The header was what helped the retrievers tell filings
    apart.

  I'll go with A unless you prefer otherwise, and pick the model, pool size and
  text from the measured numbers.

          + records what it was given."""                                
      133 +                                                              
      134 +    def __init__(self, *preferred: str) -> None:

          +st[float]:

⏺ The full retrieval eval is running now: three chunk sizes, with the new
  re-ranked mode and the latency table. It should take about 15–20 minutes, and
  I'll be notified when it's done.

  Decided so far:
  - Model: jinaai/jina-reranker-v1-tiny-en (Apache-2.0, about 130 MB, ONNX
    through fastembed, no new dependency).
  - Results at 400 tokens with 20 candidates:
    - R@6 rises from 0.83 to 0.92 on the HTML questions and from 0.76 to 0.81 on
      the TXT ones. Both are the most re-ranking 20 candidates can reach.
    - MRR rises from 0.684 to 0.715 (HTML) and from 0.496 to 0.676 (TXT).
  - Cost: about 550 ms more per question, and about 800 MB peak memory at batch
    size 8.
  - Text the re-ranker reads: the chunk with its title and section header.
    Without the header, TXT R@6 falls to 0.62, below today's baseline.
  - Gate: option A, as agreed. jina's scores overlap between answerable and
    unanswerable questions: the lowest answerable scores 1.14, while one
    unanswerable scores 1.78. So option B wouldn't have worked with this model
    anyway.
  - Not chosen:
    - bge-reranker-v2-m3-int8 ranks the evidence higher (HTML MRR 0.818), but
      takes 5.8 s and 3.3 GB per question with no better R@6.
    - bge-reranker-base takes 4.6 s with no gain over jina-tiny.
    - The full-precision v2-m3 was skipped, as you agreed.

  Built:
  - Backend: a Reranker protocol with a fastembed implementation and a fake, a
    reranked search mode, and the gate now checks the candidates before
    re-ranking.
  - API and UI: the API returns rerank_rank, and the UI shows "rerank #n" first
    in each passage's retrieval line.
  - Settings: RERANKER_MODEL and RERANK_CANDIDATES. An empty model turns
    re-ranking off.
  - Docker: the image now includes the model.
  - Eval: run_eval.py reports with and without re-ranking, and adds a latency
    table.
  - Checks: 318 backend and 31 frontend tests pass.

  Still to do: read the eval results, build the Docker image, try it in the real
  app, run the answer eval within the Groq budget, update decisions.md and the
  README, then commit and open the PR.

⏺ The re-ranker is built and committed on feat/reranker, with the docs written.
  The only thing left is the answer eval with the re-ranker on, which is about
  two-thirds of the way through its 54 questions, about 20 minutes to go.

  Result at 400 tokens: re-ranking finds three more questions in the top 6 (a25,
  n24, n02) and loses none that hybrid search had.

  ┌───────────┬────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │ Questions │ R@6 before → after │ MRR before → after │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ HTML (24) │ 0.83 → 0.92        │ 0.684 → 0.715      │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ TXT (21)  │ 0.76 → 0.81        │ 0.496 → 0.676      │
  └───────────┴────────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  That's the most re-ranking 20 candidates can reach. Each search takes about
  0.5 s more, up from 8 ms to 525 ms.

  Choices you made:
  - 20 candidates: re-ranking the top 20 after fusion. The three it can't reach
    (m01, m02, m28) were found only by BM25, so fusion ranks them below 20.
  - Option A for the gate: it still checks dense similarity, now across all
    candidates before re-ranking, so re-ranking can't change which questions are
    refused. jina's scores overlap between answerable and unanswerable
    questions, so a re-ranker gate wouldn't have worked with this model anyway.

  Choices I made from measurements:
  - Model: jinaai/jina-reranker-v1-tiny-en (Apache 2.0, 129 MB), the fastest of
    the six tried and the only small one to reach the 20-candidate ceiling. The
    two BGE models matched or ranked higher but took 4.6–5.8 s a search. The
    full-precision v2-m3 was skipped, as you agreed. The comparison table is in
    decisions.md.
  - Text it reads: the chunk with its title and section header. Without the
    header it does worse than no re-ranker on the TXT questions (R@6 0.62).
  - Batch size 8: about 800 MB peak memory instead of 1.3 GB, at the same speed.

  Checked in the real app:
  - Docker: the image builds (1.08 GB → 1.27 GB) and loads the re-ranker with
    networking off.
  - API: n24 through the dev API was retrieved in 571 ms, and the answer
    included the right figure with a citation.
  - Browser: the "rerank #n" label in the retrieval details is covered by a unit
    test only. The Chrome extension isn't connected, so it's worth a quick look
    in the UI.

  Worth a follow-up: with re-ranking, 200-token chunks find 40 of 45 questions
  in the top 6 (against 39 at 400) in about half the time. It's a one-question
  gap on the questions the settings were tuned on, so I left CHUNK_TOKENS at 400
  and added an open item to IMPROVEMENTS.md.

  Once the eval finishes, I'll score it, read every answer scored wrong, add the
  numbers to decisions.md and the README, commit, and open the PR.

❯ what is the html table column bug                                             

⏺ I'll pull the actual table from Apple's filing as the loader renders it, so
  the explanation uses the real rows.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ It's a bug in how the HTML loader turns a filing's table into text. It throws
  away every empty cell, so when a row has a blank in the middle, every cell
  after the blank slides one column to the left.

  Where it went wrong: this is Apple's share buyback table as the loader
  currently writes it.

  Periods | Total Number of Shares Purchased | Average Price Paid Per Share | 
  Total ... Under Plans or Programs | Approximate Dollar Value ... That May Yet 
  Be Purchased (1)
  Open market and privately negotiated purchases | 27,594 | $317.45 | 27,594
  ...
  Total | 81,767 | $114,004

  - What the filing means: in the Total row, the "average price" and "under
    plans" cells are blank. $114,004 (million) belongs in the last column, the
    authorization Apple still has left to spend.
  - What the loader wrote: the two blank cells were dropped, so $114,004 lands
    under "Average Price Paid Per Share", the third column.
  - What the model concluded: it read the row as "Total: 81,767 shares,
    $114,004" and reported $114 billion as the cost of the buybacks, with a
    citation. That's the a25 error from the first answer eval.

  Why the loader drops empty cells (_render_table in
  ingestion/loaders/html.py:123): SEC inline-XBRL tables are full of empty
  spacer cells, and they put $, ) and % in cells of their own. Dropping empty
  cells and merging the symbols turns a row like Net sales | | $ | 1,234 | | ...
  into a readable Net sales | $1,234. That works for rows with every value
  filled in, but it silently misaligns rows with gaps.

  The fix I'd make: keep each cell in its real column, including cells that span
  several columns (colspan). Then drop only the columns that are empty in every
  row, which are the spacers. A blank in one row stays as an empty cell, so the
  Total row would come out as Total | 81,767 | | | $114,004. The $/)/% merging
  stays as it is.

  Testing it:
  - Unit tests: a test with a row that has gaps, plus the existing table tests.
  - Measure: how many table rows in the six filings change.
  - Re-run both evals: the answer eval only re-asks questions whose retrieved
    text changed.

  One side effect: rows with gaps get a little longer, so a few chunk boundaries
  may move.

✻ Worked for 19s · done 12:39 AM
